We study tensor products of two-dimensional evaluation U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} -modules at generic values of q , U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} -homomorphisms between them, and closely related subjects. A Proofs. B Tables of conf-connected words of lengths 4,6,8,10 up to symmetries. References 1. Introduction. The representation theory of quantum groups has been intensively studied for the last four decades with many amazing findings and beautiful results. Many efforts have been made to understand irreducible finite-dimensional modules over affine quantum groups for generic values of q and to study the root of unity phenomenon. Many authors study super-symmetric versions, shifted quantum groups, quantum toroidal algebras, etc. However, many questions are still unanswered even in the simplest cases. The current project was started by the question posed by C. Stroppel to the second author a couple of years ago: Given a tensor product w of several two-dimensional evaluation modules of U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} , what is h(w)=\dim\big(\mathrm{Hom}_{U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}}(\mathbb{C},w)\big)? Although there are many other amusing questions one can ask about tensor products w of evaluation modules of U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} , see Section 11.4 , the problem of finding ways to compute h(w) is an overarching motif of our work. One can restrict to evaluation parameters of the form q^{a} , a\in 2\mathbb{Z} , see Proposition 3.8 . We denote the tensor product of 2 -dimensional evaluation modules with evaluation parameters q^{a_{i}} simply by the word w=a_{1}a_{2}...a_{k} , where a_{i} are even integers. We call k the length of w . We call the word w connected if the set of all distinct letters a_{i} is of the form \{a,a+2,\dots,a+2m\} for some a,m . One can further reduce to the case of connected words of even length, see Proposition 4.12 . Then for each connected word of even length we have the number h(w)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0} . For example, one can compute h(220200242424)=4. The number h(w) depends on the order of the letters in w . For example, if a_{1}\geq a_{i} for all i then h(w)=0 (for our choice of the coproduct). On the other hand h(w) for a word of length 2n can be as large as (2-\epsilon)^{n} , see Proposition 8.8 . The statistics h(w) contains information about dimensions of all homomorphism spaces between any tensor products of 2 -dimensional evaluation modules \mathrm{Hom}_{U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}}(w_{1},w_{2}) , see Lemma 3.1 and Lemma 3.6 . The singular vectors of weight zero in a tensor product of 2n copies of the U_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2} 2-dimensional module have a basis which is identified with Catalan arc configurations - the sets of non-intersecting arcs in half-plane connecting 2n points on a line. Such objects are related to Temperley-Lieb algebras and used in the categorification of U_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2} , see [ BFK99 ] , [ FKSt07 ] . One could ask if we can construct a basis of U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} singular vectors in w in a similar fashion. Clearly, in the affine case the arcs should connect letter a and a+2 since the only words of length 2 which have a trivial submodule are of the form a(a+2) . The non-intersecting property also has to be relaxed as we have too few arcs. Here is an example of an arc configuration. 2 2 0 2 0 0 2 4 2 4 2 4 Indeed, we prove a number of results which support this idea: (1) h(w)\neq 0 if and only if there exists an arc configuration, see Theorem 5.25 ; (2) h(w) is at least the number of irreducible arc configurations, see Theorem 5.6 ; (3) h(w) is at most the number of steady arc configurations, see Theorem 5.20 . The irreducible arc configurations do not have intersecting arcs connecting the same letters a and a+2 , and if an arc connecting a with a+2 intersects with an arc connecting a+2 with a+4 then the latter should be on the left, see Definition 5.1 . The example of the arc configuration we give above is irreducible. The definition of the steady arc configurations is slightly more technical, see Definition 5.18 . For the example of the word above, the number of irreducible and steady arc configurations are both equal to 4 which shows h(w)=4 . Alternatively, one can show that numbers h(w) respect several symmetries: h(a_{1}a_{2}...a_{k})=h(a_{2}...a_{k}(a_{1}+4))=h((-a_{k})...(-a_{1}))=h((a_{1}+b)...(a_{k}+b)). It is also easy to find the number of trivial components of the Jordan-Holder series of a word by the q -character method, see Lemma 5.10 and Proposition 5.11 . Finally, for a short exact sequence \mathrm{U}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{V}\twoheadrightarrow\mathrm{W} we always have h(\mathrm{V})\leq h(\mathrm{U})+h(\mathrm{W}) . Such tools are sufficient to find h(w)=4 in our example and many others, see Section 8.1 . One can treat even more cases if the two approaches are combined and enhanced by using the isomorphisms between words, see Section 8.3 . Neither irreducible nor steady arc configurations provide sharp bounds in general. We expect that the condition on arcs which produces exactly h(w) arc configurations is not described by pairwise interactions of arcs, and that it is not local. As an illustration of the non-local nature of the problem, we study the isomorphisms between different words. We certainly have ab=ba unless |a-b|=2 . However, there are other isomorphism which cannot be deduced from pairs of letters. For example, 0020\cong 0200 , 000200\cong 002000 . We expect 0^{k+1}2^{k}0^{k}\cong 0^{k}2^{k}0^{k+1} for all k , see Section 8.2 . It is a very interesting and challenging question to classify words up to isomorphisms. We conjecture such a classification for words whose letters are 0 and 2 , see Conjecture 7.6 . Since the number of trivial composition factors in w is easy to compute, the main difficulty in computing h(w) is that the words are not semi-simple modules. We try to develop a theory of indecomposable modules introducing graphs which enhance the socle filtration, see Section 10 . Essentially h(w) for a word w is a nullity of linear map f_{0}\in U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} sending the subspace of U_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2} singular vectors of weight zero to the subspace of U_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2} singular vectors of weight two. For a word of length 2n , given a basis, f_{0} becomes a matrix of size \frac{3}{n+2}\binom{2n}{n-1}\times\frac{1}{n+1}\binom{2n}{n} . A straightforward Mathematica program computes h(w) for words up to length 12 and even 14 (using the Yangian version). Moreover, a computer can easily calculate the upper and lower bounds up to length 22 and often these bounds coincide. So, one can generate data and try to approach the problem completely combinatorially or using machine learning. We give all essential data (up to symmetries) for words up to length 10 in Appendix B . We believe that h(w) can be computed directly from the combinatorial set of all arc configurations, see Conjecture 6.23 . However, we did not manage to find such an algorithm; there may exists a more suitable combinatorics. If letters a_{i} in w are non-decreasing, the word w is known as U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} Weyl module. The Weyl modules have been studied for a long time, see [ Cha02 ] . Our bounds recover the results of [ BC25 ] on maps between Weyl modules in the case of \mathfrak{sl}_{2} , see Proposition 8.7 . Arbitrary words are also called U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} mixed Weyl modules, much less is known about mixed case. We also hope that this paper can be of some educational value as we have collected many facts and methods scattered in the literature in the simplest non-trivial case of U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} . For that purpose, we provide a lot of examples and give detailed proofs. At the end we give a list of questions for possible further work, see Section 11.4 . The paper is constructed as follows. In Sections 2 and 3 we collect the known facts about U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} and its finite-dimensional representations. In Section 4 we start the study of numbers of h(w) . We develop the language of arc configurations and prove a few properties of h(w) statistics. In Section 5 we prove the upper and lower bounds for h(w) and prove that h(w)\neq 0 if and only is the set of arc configurations is non-empty. In Section 6 we study our problem from the point of view of hyperplane arrangements. In this section we study numbers h(w) using degenerations from generic words. In Section 7 we address the problem of classifying words up to isomorphism. In Section 8 we show how our theory works in various explicit examples. In particular, the example in the introduction can be rather easily computed by hand. Section 9 is devoted to the study of extensions. In particular, we classify all extensions between two evaluation modules. In Section 10 we attempt to visualize words using graphs build on socle filtration, which we call submodule graphs. In Section 11 we discuss combinatorics corresponding to singular vectors and list a number of unanswered questions related to words. 2. Preliminaries. In this section we recall well-known facts about the affine quantum group U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} (at level zero) and the corresponding Yangian \mathrm{Y}\mathfrak{sl}_{2} . 2.1. The quantum group U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} . Fix a non-zero complex number q which is not a root of unity. We fix a value of \log q , so that q^{a} is defined for all a\in\mathbb{C} . The quantum group U_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2} is the unital associative algebra with generators e,f,K^{\pm 1} and relations KK^{-1}=K^{-1}K=1,\;\;KfK^{-1}=q^{-2}f,\;\;KeK^{-1}=q^{2}e,\;\;[e,f]=\frac{K-K^{-1}}{q-q^{-1}}. The affine quantum group \mathfrak{sl}_{2} in Drinfeld-Jimbo realization is given as follows. Definition 2.1 . The affine quantum group U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} at level zero is the unital associative algebra with generators e_{i},f_{i},K^{\pm 1} , i=0,1 , and defining relations (2.1a) KK^{-1}=K^{-1}K=1, (2.1b) Kf_{1}K^{-1}=q^{-2}f_{1},\;\;Ke_{1}K^{-1}=q^{2}e_{1},\;\;Kf_{0}K^{-1}=q^{2}f_{0},\;\;Ke_{0}K^{-1}=q^{-2}e_{0}, (2.1c) [e_{1},f_{1}]=-[e_{0},f_{0}]=\frac{K-K^{-1}}{q-q^{-1}}, (2.1d) [e_{1},f_{0}]=[e_{0},f_{1}]=0, (2.1e) \displaystyle e_{i}^{3}e_{j}-(q^{2}+1+q^{-2})e_{i}^{2}e_{j}e_{i}+(q^{2}+1+q^{-2})e_{i}e_{j}e_{i}^{2}-e_{j}e_{i}^{3} \displaystyle=0, (2.1f) \displaystyle f_{i}^{3}f_{j}-(q^{2}+1+q^{-2})f_{i}^{2}f_{j}f_{i}+(q^{2}+1+q^{-2})f_{i}f_{j}f_{i}^{2}-f_{j}f_{i}^{3} \displaystyle=0,\;\;i,j\in\{0,1\},\;i\neq j. We use the Hopf algebra structure on U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} with comultiplication \Delta , antipode S , and counit \epsilon given by (2.2) \begin{aligned} \Delta:U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}&\longrightarrow U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}\otimes U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2},\\ K^{\pm 1}&\longmapsto K^{\pm 1}\otimes K^{\pm 1},\\ e_{0}&\longmapsto e_{0}\otimes K^{-1}+1\otimes e_{0},\\ f_{0}&\longmapsto f_{0}\otimes 1+K\otimes f_{0},\\ e_{1}&\longmapsto e_{1}\otimes K+1\otimes e_{1},\\ f_{1}&\longmapsto f_{1}\otimes 1+K^{-1}\otimes f_{1},\\ \end{aligned}\qquad\begin{aligned} S:U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}&\longrightarrow U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2},\\ K^{\pm 1}&\longmapsto K^{\mp 1},\\ e_{0}&\longmapsto-e_{0}K,\\ f_{0}&\longmapsto-K^{-1}f_{0},\\ e_{1}&\longmapsto-e_{1}K^{-1},\\ f_{1}&\longmapsto-Kf_{1},\\ \end{aligned}\qquad\begin{aligned} \varepsilon:U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}&\longrightarrow\mathbb{C},\\ K^{\pm 1}&\longmapsto 1,\\ e_{0}&\longmapsto 0,\\ f_{0}&\longmapsto 0,\\ e_{1}&\longmapsto 0,\\ f_{1}&\longmapsto 0.\end{aligned} There are two embeddings of U_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2} to U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} given by (2.3) \begin{aligned} \iota_{1}:U_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}&\longrightarrow U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2},\\ K^{\pm 1}&\longmapsto K^{\pm 1},\\ e&\longmapsto e_{1},\\ f&\longmapsto f_{1},\end{aligned}\qquad\begin{aligned} \iota_{0}:U_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}&\longrightarrow U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2},\\ K^{\pm 1}&\longmapsto K^{\mp 1},\\ e&\longmapsto e_{0},\\ f&\longmapsto f_{0}.\end{aligned} Denote the images of embeddings \iota_{j} by U_{q}^{j}\mathfrak{sl}_{2} , j=0,1 . The algebras U_{q}^{j}\mathfrak{sl}_{2} are Hopf subalgebras of U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} . We often write simply U_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}\subset U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} instead of U_{q}^{1}\mathfrak{sl}_{2} . Conversely, there exists a family of evaluation surjective homomorphisms from U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} to U_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2} parameterized by complex number a\in\mathbb{C} , (2.4) \displaystyle\mathrm{ev}_{a}:U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}\longrightarrow U_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}, \displaystyle K^{\pm 1}\longmapsto K^{\pm 1}, \displaystyle e_{0}\longmapsto q^{a}f,\hskip 10.0ptf_{0}\longmapsto q^{-a}e, \displaystyle e_{1}\longmapsto e,\hskip 22.0ptf_{1}\longmapsto f. The evaluation map ev_{a} is not a homomorphism of Hopf algebras. Clearly, \mathrm{ev}_{a}\circ\iota_{1}=id . Note that in our notation we use simply a instead of q^{a} . As a result, \mathrm{ev}_{a}=\mathrm{ev}_{a+\frac{2\pi\mathrm{i}}{\log(q)}} . A similar ambiguity will occur often. We hope that it does not lead to any confusion. The algebra U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} can alternatively be written in a new Drinfeld form. In the new Drinfeld realization the algebra U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} has generators \{x_{r}^{\pm},h_{n},K^{\pm 1},\} collected in series \psi^{\pm}(z),x^{\pm}(z) of a formal variable z (2.5) \psi^{\pm}(z)=\sum_{r=0}^{\infty}\psi_{\pm r}^{\pm}z^{\pm r}=K^{\pm 1}\exp\left(\pm(q-q^{-1})\sum_{s=1}^{\infty}h_{\pm s}z^{\pm s}\right),\qquad x^{\pm}(z)=\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}}x_{r}^{\pm}z^{r}. The defining relations are (2.6a) \displaystyle\psi^{\pm}(z)\psi^{\pm}(w)=\psi^{\pm}(w)\psi^{\pm}(z),\hskip 10.0pt\psi^{\pm}(z)\psi^{\mp}(w)=\psi^{\mp}(w)\psi^{\pm}(z), (2.6b) \displaystyle(w-zq^{\pm 2})x^{\pm}(z)x^{\pm}(w)+(z-wq^{\pm 2})x^{\pm}(w)x^{\pm}(z)=0, (2.6c) \displaystyle[x^{+}(z),x^{-}(w)]=\frac{1}{q-q^{-1}}\left(\delta(z/w)\psi^{+}(z)-\delta(z/w)\psi^{-}(w)\right), (2.6d) \displaystyle\psi^{+}(z)x^{\pm}(w)=\left(q^{2}\frac{w-q^{-2}z}{w-q^{2}z}\right)^{\pm 1}x^{\pm}(w)\psi^{+}(z), (2.6e) \displaystyle\psi^{-}(z)x^{\pm}(w)=\left(q^{2}\frac{w-q^{-2}z}{w-q^{2}z}\right)^{\pm 1}x^{\pm}(w)\psi^{-}(z). Here \delta(z/w)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\left(\frac{z}{w}\right)^{n}. An isomorphism between the Drinfeld-Jimbo and new Drinfeld realizations is given on generators by (2.7) \displaystyle K^{\pm 1}\longmapsto K^{\pm 1}, \displaystyle e_{0}\longmapsto x_{1}^{-},\hskip 30.0ptf_{0}\longmapsto x_{-1}^{+}, \displaystyle e_{1}\longmapsto K^{-1}x_{0}^{+},\hskip 8.0ptf_{1}\longmapsto x_{0}^{-}K. The comultipilication ( 2.2 ) written in the new Drinfeld realization has the property (2.8) \Delta(\psi^{\pm}(z))=\psi^{\pm}(z)\otimes\psi^{\pm}(z)\operatorname{mod}(U^{\pm}\otimes U^{\mp}), where U^{\pm}=\underset{k>0}{\bigoplus}U_{\pm 2k} with U_{l}=\{u\in U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}:KuK^{-1}=q^{l}u\} being the subspace of elements of weight l . There is a family of shift automorphisms \tau_{a} of Hopf algebra U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} parameterized by a complex number a , (2.9) \displaystyle\tau_{a}:U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}\longrightarrow U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}, \displaystyle K^{\pm 1}\longmapsto K^{\pm 1}, \displaystyle e_{0}\longmapsto q^{a}e_{0},\hskip 10.0ptf_{0}\longmapsto q^{-a}f_{0}, \displaystyle e_{1}\longmapsto e_{1},\hskip 22.0ptf_{1}\longmapsto f_{1}, or, in new Drinfeld realization, (2.10) \displaystyle\psi^{\pm}(z) \displaystyle\longmapsto\psi^{\pm}(q^{a}z), \displaystyle x^{\pm}(z) \displaystyle\longmapsto x^{\pm}(q^{a}z). Clearly, \mathrm{ev}_{b}\circ\tau_{a}=\mathrm{ev}_{a+b} . 2.2. The U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} -modules and q -characters. The category of finite-dimensional U_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2} -modules is semi-simple. We denote the irreducible U_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2} -module of type 1 and of dimension m+1 by \mathrm{L}_{m} , m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0} . The module \mathrm{L}_{m} has a basis \{v_{0},\dots,v_{m}\} such that K\,v_{i}=q^{2(m-i)}v_{i},\qquad f\,v_{i}=[i+1]_{q}v_{i+1},\qquad e\,v_{i}=[m-i+1]_{q}v_{i-1}. Here, v_{-1}=v_{m+1}=0 and [i]_{q}=\frac{q^{i}-q^{-i}}{q-q^{-1}}. In general, given a U_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2} -module \mathrm{V} , a vector v\in\mathrm{V} is called a weight vector of weight a if K\,v=q^{a}v . A weight vector v\in\mathrm{V} is called singular if e\,v=0 . The module \rm{L}_{m} is generated by a singular vector v_{0} of weight m . The category of finite-dimensional U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} -modules is not semi-simple. All U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} -modules in this paper are finite-dimensional and are of type 1 . In all statements we assume the finite-dimensionality and type 1 without mentioning. For a\in\mathbb{C} , define a U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} -module structure on \mathrm{L}_{m} by a map (2.11) \rho_{\mathrm{L}_{m}}\circ\mathrm{ev}_{a}:U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}\longrightarrow\mathrm{End}_{\mathbb{C}}\left(\mathrm{L}_{m}\right). The resulting U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} -module is called the evaluation module with the evaluation parameter a . Motivated by q -characters (see below) we denote the module defined by ( 2.11 ) by the string [\alpha,\beta] , where \alpha=a-m+1 , \beta=a+m-1 . We note that in the notation [\alpha,\beta] we always assume that \beta-\alpha\in 2\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0} , then m=\frac{1}{2}(\beta-\alpha)+1 is the highest weight and m+1 is the dimension. The evaluation parameter is \frac{1}{2}(\alpha+\beta) . For a tensor product of evaluation modules instead of [\alpha_{1}{,}\beta_{1}]{\otimes}[\alpha_{2}{,}\beta_{2}] we write simply [\alpha_{1}{,}\beta_{1}][\alpha_{2}{,}\beta_{2}] . Two strings [\alpha_{1},\beta_{1}] and [\alpha_{2},\beta_{2}] are in general position if either one string contains the other, (2.12) \{\alpha_{1},\dots,\beta_{1}\}\subseteq\{\alpha_{2},\dots,\beta_{2}\}\textit{, or }\{\alpha_{2},\dots,\beta_{2}\}\subseteq\{\alpha_{1},\dots,\beta_{1}\}, or the union of the strings is not a string, \{\alpha_{1},\dots,\beta_{1}\}\cap\{\alpha_{2}-2,\alpha_{2},\dots,\beta_{2},\beta_{2}+2\}=\varnothing. A tensor product of evaluation modules is irreducible if and only if the corresponding strings are in pairwise general position. Two irreducible products of evaluation modules are isomorphic if and only if they differ only by a permutation of factors. Every irreducible U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} -module is a tensor product of evaluation modules, see [ CP91 ] . A vector v in a U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} -module \mathrm{V} is called an \ell -weight vector of \ell -weight \phi^{\pm}(z)\in\mathbb{C}[[z^{\pm 1}]] if \psi^{\pm}(z)v=\phi^{\pm}(z)v . A vector v is called a generalized \ell -weight vector of \ell -weight \phi^{\pm}(z) if (\psi^{\pm}(z)-\phi^{\pm}(z))^{\dim\mathrm{V}}v=0 . An \ell -weight vector v is called \ell -singular if x^{+}(z)v=0 . If \phi^{\pm}(z) is an \ell -weight of a vector in a U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} -module then there exists a rational function \phi(z) such that \phi(0)\phi(\infty)=1 and \phi^{+}(z) and \phi^{-}(z) are expansions of \phi(z) at z=0 and z=\infty , respectively. For a rational function \phi(z) denote the space of generalized vectors of \ell -weight \phi in \mathrm{V} by \mathrm{V}[\phi] . The q -character of \mathrm{V} is the formal sum of \ell -weights of \mathrm{V} given by \chi_{q}(\mathrm{V})=\sum_{\phi}\dim\left(\mathrm{V}[\phi]\right)\phi. We use the notation 1_{a} , a\in\mathbb{C} , for a rational function 1_{a}=q\,\frac{1-q^{a-1}z}{1-q^{a+1}z}. The \ell -weights in U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} -modules are monomials in 1_{a}^{\pm 1} . Thus, the q -character is a Laurent polynomial in commutative variables 1_{a} with non-negative integer coefficients. For a short exact triple of U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} modules \mathrm{V}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{U}\twoheadrightarrow\mathrm{W} , \chi_{q}(\mathrm{U})=\chi_{q}(\mathrm{V})+\chi_{q}(\mathrm{W}). Due to triangularity ( 2.8 ), the current \Delta(\psi)(z)-\psi(z)\otimes\psi(z) is nilpotent in a product \mathrm{V}\otimes\mathrm{W} of U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} -modules. Thus \chi_{q}(\mathrm{V}\otimes\mathrm{W})=\chi_{q}(\mathrm{V})\chi_{q}(\mathrm{W}). The q -character is an injective ring homomorphism from the Grothendieck ring of finite-dimensional U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} -modules to the ring of Laurent polynomials in variables 1_{a} , a\in\mathbb{C} . A monomial of a q -character 1_{a_{1}}^{n_{1}}\dots 1_{a_{k}}^{n_{k}} is called dominant if all n_{k} are non-negative integers. An irreducible U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} -module is generated by an \ell -singular vector. An \ell -weight of an \ell -singular vector is a dominant monomial. We call a U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} -module \mathrm{V} thin if all \ell -weight subspaces of \mathrm{V} are one-dimensional. In other words \mathrm{V} is thin if coefficients of all monomials in \chi_{q}(\mathrm{V}) are one. An evaluation module \mathrm{V}=[\alpha,\beta] is thin and \ell -weights are given by (2.13) q^{m-2j}\frac{\left(1-q^{\alpha-1}z\right)\left(1-q^{\beta+3}z\right)}{\left(1-q^{\beta+1-2j}z\right)\left(1-q^{\beta+3-2j}z\right)},\;\;j=0,\dots,m. In particular, the q -character of an evaluation module \mathrm{V}=[\alpha,\beta] is given by (2.14) \chi_{q}(\mathrm{V})=\sum_{j=0}^{m}q^{m-2j}\frac{\left(1-q^{\alpha-1}z\right)\left(1-q^{\beta+3}z\right)}{\left(1-q^{\beta+1-2j}z\right)\left(1-q^{\beta+3-2j}z\right)}=\sum_{j=0}^{m}1_{\alpha}1_{\alpha+2}\dots 1_{\beta-2j}1_{\beta-2j+4}^{-1}1_{\beta-2j+6}^{-1}\dots 1_{\beta}^{-1}1_{\beta+2}^{-1}, where m=(\beta-\alpha)/2+1 . For example, the q -character of two-dimensional evaluation module [a,a] is \chi_{q}([a,a])=1_{a}+1_{a+2}^{-1}. The dominant monomial of \chi_{q}([\alpha,\beta]) , 1_{\alpha}1_{\alpha+2}\dots 1_{\beta-2}1_{\beta} , is reflected in our notation for evaluation modules. In particular, q -characters of irreducible U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} -modules are explicit. Moreover, given \chi_{q}(\mathrm{V}) , one can explicitly find the composition factors of \mathrm{V} . In other words, we can find the class of \mathrm{V} in the Grothendieck ring of the category of finite-dimensional representations of U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} . A monomial in a q -character is called right-negative if it contains 1_{a}^{-m} , for some a\in\mathbb{C} , m\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0} , and does not contain 1_{a+2k} with k\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0} in any power. A product of two right negative monomials is right negative. All non-dominant monomials in the q -character ( 2.14 ) of an evaluation module are right negative. In particular, if a product of two monomials in q -characters of evaluation modules is dominant, then at least one of the monomials is dominant. Example 2.2 . Let \alpha_{1},\beta_{1} \alpha_{2},\beta_{2} be four even integers such that \alpha_{1}<\alpha_{2}\leq\beta_{1}+2\leq\beta_{2} . Then the dominant monomials in \chi_{q}([\alpha_{1},\beta_{1}][\alpha_{2},\beta_{2}]) are 1_{\alpha_{1}}\dots 1_{\beta_{1}}1_{\alpha_{2}}\dots 1_{\beta_{2}},\ 1_{\alpha_{1}}\dots 1_{\beta_{1}-2}1_{\alpha_{2}}\dots\widehat{1_{\beta_{1}+2}}\dots 1_{\beta_{2}},\ \\ 1_{\alpha_{1}}\dots 1_{\beta_{1}-4}1_{\alpha_{2}}\dots\widehat{1_{\beta_{1}}}\widehat{1_{\beta_{1}+2}}\dots 1_{\beta_{2}},\ \dots\ ,\ 1_{\alpha_{1}}\dots 1_{\alpha_{2}-4}1_{\beta_{1}+4},\dots,1_{\beta_{2}}. 2.3. Drinfeld coproduct and universal R -matrix. The quantum group U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} is known to be quasi-triangular. It means that there exists an invertible \mathcal{R}\in U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}\hat{\otimes}U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} such that \mathcal{R}\Delta(x)\mathcal{R}^{-1}=\Delta^{op}(x),\qquad\forall x\in U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}, and (2.15) (\Delta\otimes 1)(\mathcal{R})=\mathcal{R}_{13}\mathcal{R}_{23},\;\;(1\otimes\Delta)(\mathcal{R})=\mathcal{R}_{13}\mathcal{R}_{12}. Here \Delta^{op}(x)={\mathop{\sum}_{i=1}^{m}}b_{i}\otimes a_{i} , whenever \Delta(x)={\mathop{\sum}_{i=1}^{m}}a_{i}\otimes b_{i} . The element \mathcal{R} is called the universal R -matrix. If the series defining \mathcal{R} converges in \mathrm{End}(\mathrm{V}\otimes\mathrm{W}) , then \check{\mathcal{R}}:\mathrm{V}\otimes\mathrm{W}\to\mathrm{W}\otimes\mathrm{V} , where \check{\mathcal{R}}=P\mathcal{R} , is a U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} -homomorphism. Here P:\mathrm{V}\otimes\mathrm{W}\rightarrow\mathrm{W}\otimes\mathrm{V} is the permutation operator. Equations ( 2.15 ) allow to compute the action of \mathcal{R} on \mathrm{V}\otimes(\mathrm{W}_{1}\otimes\mathrm{W}_{2}) (respectively, (\mathrm{W}_{1}\otimes\mathrm{W}_{2})\otimes\mathrm{V} ) as long as the action of \mathcal{R} on \mathrm{V}\otimes\mathrm{W}_{1} , \mathrm{V}\otimes\mathrm{W}_{2} (respectively, \mathrm{W}_{1}\otimes\mathrm{V} , \mathrm{W}_{2}\otimes\mathrm{V} ) is defined. The universal \mathcal{R} -matrix admits the decomposition (2.16) \mathcal{R}=q^{h_{0}\otimes h_{0}}\mathcal{R}_{-}\mathcal{R}_{0}\mathcal{R}_{+}. Here \mathcal{R}_{\pm}-1 is in a completion of U^{\pm}\otimes U^{\mp} and h_{0}=\log(K)/\log(q) . We list explicit formulas for \mathcal{R}_{+},\mathcal{R}_{-},\mathcal{R}_{0} in Appendix A . The (normalized) action of \check{\mathcal{R}} in [\alpha_{1},\beta_{1}]\otimes[\alpha_{2},\beta_{2}] is given in Proposition 2.7 , cf. also ( 4.5 ). Drinfeld coproduct \Delta^{(D)} is an algebra homomorphism from U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} to a completion of U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}\otimes U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} given by \displaystyle\psi^{\pm}(z) \displaystyle\mapsto\psi^{\pm}(z){\otimes}\psi^{\pm}(z), (2.17) \displaystyle x^{+}(z) \displaystyle\mapsto x^{+}(z)\otimes\psi^{-}(z)+1\otimes x^{+}(z), \displaystyle x^{-}(z) \displaystyle\mapsto x^{-}(z)\otimes 1+\psi^{+}(z)\otimes x^{-}(z). If \mathrm{V},\mathrm{W} are U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} -modules and the series converge in \mathrm{V}\otimes\mathrm{W} , then the Drinfeld coproduct gives a U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} -module structure on \mathrm{V}\otimes\mathrm{W} . We denote this module \mathrm{V}\otimes_{D}\mathrm{W} . Note that in some cases \mathrm{V}\otimes_{D}\mathrm{W} is not defined. It is known that the Drinfeld coproduct is related to the comultiplication ( 2.2 ) by (2.18) \Delta^{(D)}(x)=\mathcal{R}_{+}\Delta(x)\mathcal{R}_{+}^{-1},\qquad\forall x\in U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}, see [ EKP07 ] . Thus, if the action of \mathcal{R}_{+} on \mathrm{V}\otimes\mathrm{W} and \mathrm{V}\otimes_{D}\mathrm{W} is well-defined, then \mathrm{V}\otimes\mathrm{W}\cong\mathrm{V}\otimes_{D}\mathrm{W} . The isomorphism is given by the action of \mathcal{R}_{+}^{-1} on \mathrm{V}\otimes\mathrm{W} . If \mathrm{V}\otimes_{D}\mathrm{W} is well-defined and irreducible, then \mathrm{V}\otimes\mathrm{W}\cong\mathrm{V}\otimes_{D}\mathrm{W} . We expect that this is a general fact. Conjecture 2.3 . If \mathrm{V}\otimes_{D}\mathrm{W} is well-defined, then \mathrm{V}\otimes\mathrm{W}\cong\mathrm{V}\otimes_{D}\mathrm{W} . We prove the conjecture in a special case of thin modules. Definition 2.4 . A pair of U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} -modules is called q -character separated if for any pair of \ell -weights 1_{a_{1}}^{m_{1}}\dots 1_{a_{k}}^{m_{k}} , 1_{b_{1}}^{n_{1}}\dots 1_{b_{l}}^{n_{l}} in q-characters \chi_{q}(\mathrm{V}) and \chi_{q}(\mathrm{W}) respectively, a_{i}\neq b_{j} for any i,j . Proposition 2.5 . Let U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} -modules \mathrm{V} and \mathrm{W} be thin and q -character separated. Let the action of universal R-matrix \mathcal{R} be defined on \mathrm{V}\otimes\mathrm{W} . Then \mathrm{V}\otimes_{D}\mathrm{W} is well-defined and \mathrm{V}\otimes_{D}\mathrm{W}\cong\mathrm{V}\otimes\mathrm{W} . The proof is given in Appendix A . We expect that the condition of \mathrm{V} and \mathrm{W} being thin in Proposition 2.5 can be dropped. Particular case of our interest is the following Corollary 2.6 . Let \mathrm{V}=\otimes_{i=1}^{k}[\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}],\;\mathrm{W}=\otimes_{j=1}^{l}[\alpha^{\prime}_{j},\beta^{\prime}_{j}] be such that \{\alpha_{i},\dots\beta_{i}\}\cap\{\alpha_{j},\dots\beta_{j}\}=\varnothing and \{\alpha_{i}^{\prime},\dots\beta_{i}^{\prime}\}\cap\{\alpha_{j}^{\prime},\dots\beta_{j}^{\prime}\}=\varnothing whenever i\neq j . Assume further that for any i,j and for any a\in\{\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i}+2,\dots,\beta_{i}-2,\beta_{i}\},\;\;b\in\{\alpha^{\prime}_{j},\alpha_{j}^{\prime}+2,\dots,\beta^{\prime}_{j}-2,\beta^{\prime}_{j}\} , we have a-b\notin\{-2,0,2\} . Then \mathrm{V}\otimes_{D}\mathrm{W}\cong\mathrm{V}\otimes\mathrm{W} . Proof. The first two conditions guarantee that \mathrm{V} and \mathrm{W} are thin. The last two conditions make sure \mathrm{V} and \mathrm{W} are q -character separated and that the R -matrix is well-defined. Corollary 2.6 follows from Proposition 2.5 . ∎ 2.4. \check{R} -matrix homomorphisms. The space of U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} -homomorphisms between tensor products of two evaluation modules in different orders is always one-dimensional. We describe it explicitly. As U_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2} -modules, (2.19) [\alpha_{1},\beta_{1}][\alpha_{2},\beta_{2}]\underset{U_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}{\cong}\mathrm{L}_{m}\otimes\mathrm{L}_{n}\underset{U_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}{\cong}\mathrm{L}_{m+n}\oplus\mathrm{L}_{m+n-2}\oplus\dots\oplus\mathrm{L}_{|m-n|+2}\oplus\mathrm{L}_{|m-n|}, where m=(\beta_{1}-\alpha_{1})/2+1 , n=(\beta_{2}-\alpha_{2})/2+1 . Embedding \mathrm{L}_{m+n-2k}\subseteq\mathrm{L}_{m}\otimes\mathrm{L}_{n} is defined up to a constant. We fix the constant by choosing a singular vector (2.20) u_{k}=\sum_{i,j:\ i+j=k}(-1)^{j}q^{(n-j+1)j}[m-i]_{q}![n-j]_{q}!(f^{(i)}v_{0})\otimes(f^{(j)}w_{0})\in\mathrm{L}_{m}\otimes\mathrm{L}_{n}. Here, [n]_{q}!=\prod_{j=1}^{n}[j]_{q} , f^{(l)}=\frac{f^{l}}{[l]_{q}!} , v_{0} and w_{0} are singular vectors in \mathrm{L}_{m} and \mathrm{L}_{n} , respectively. Let u_{k}^{op}\in\mathrm{L}_{n}\otimes\mathrm{L}_{m} be the vector of the form ( 2.20 ) where i is interchanged with j , and m with n . We identify \mathrm{L}_{m}\otimes\mathrm{L}_{n} and \mathrm{L}_{n}\otimes\mathrm{L}_{m} as vector spaces by identifying f^{l}u_{k} with f^{l}u_{k}^{\text{op}} for all k,l . Any U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} -homomorphism is of the form (2.21) \check{R}=\lambda_{0}\mathrm{Id}_{\mathrm{L}_{m+n}}\oplus\lambda_{1}\mathrm{Id}_{\mathrm{L}_{m+n-2}}\oplus\dots\oplus\lambda_{\min\{m,n\}}\mathrm{Id}_{\mathrm{L}_{|m-n|}}. Proposition 2.7 . The operator \check{R}:[\alpha_{1},\beta_{1}][\alpha_{2},\beta_{2}]\longrightarrow[\alpha_{2},\beta_{2}][\alpha_{1},\beta_{1}] , defined by ( 2.21 ) is an U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} -homomorphism if and only if (2.22) \lambda_{k}=c\prod_{l=0}^{k-1}\left(1-zq^{m+n-2l}\right)\prod_{l=k}^{\min\{m,n\}-1}\left(z-q^{m+n-2l}\right). Here c\in\mathbb{C} , z=q^{a-b} , where a=\alpha_{1}+m-1,\;\;b=\alpha_{2}+n-1 are the evaluation parameters. This statement is known (see for example [ CP95 ] ). For completeness we provide a proof in Appendix A . Due to ( 2.15 ), Proposition 2.7 allows us to compute R -matrix acting on a tensor product of two arbitrary tensor products of evaluation modules. 2.5. The Yangian \mathrm{Y}\mathfrak{sl}_{2} . We define the Yangian \mathrm{Y}\mathfrak{sl}_{2} and discuss sets of generators. We start with the \mathrm{Y}\mathfrak{gl}_{2} . Definition 2.8 . The \mathrm{Y}\mathfrak{gl}_{2} is an associative unital algebra with generators t_{ij}^{(r)} , i,j=1,2 , r\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0} , collected in series t_{ij}(u) in a formal variable u , t_{ij}(u)=\delta_{ij}+\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}t_{ij}^{(r)}u^{-r}, and defining relations (2.23) (u-v)[t_{ij}(u),t_{kl}(v)]=t_{kj}(u)t_{il}(v)-t_{kj}(v)t_{il}(u). In modes the defining relations take the form (2.24) [t_{ij}^{(r+1)},t_{kl}^{(s)}]-[t_{ij}^{(r)},t_{kl}^{(s+1)}]=t_{ij}^{(r)}t_{kl}^{(s)}-t_{ij}^{(s)}t_{kl}^{(r)}. The defining relations can also be written in a matrix form R(u-v)T_{1}(u)T_{2}(v)=T_{2}(v)T_{1}(u)R(u-v),\qquad T(u)=\begin{pmatrix}t_{11}(u)&t_{12}(u)\\ t_{21}(u)&t_{22}(u)\end{pmatrix}, where T_{1}(u)=T(u)\otimes 1 , T_{2}(u)=1\otimes T(u) , R(u)=1-Pu^{-1} . We use the Hopf algebra structure on \mathrm{Y}\mathfrak{sl}_{2} with comultiplication \Delta , antipode S , and counit \epsilon given by (2.25) \displaystyle\Delta:\mathrm{Y}\mathfrak{gl}_{2} \displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathrm{Y}\mathfrak{gl}_{2}\otimes\mathrm{Y}\mathfrak{gl}_{2},\qquad \displaystyle S:\mathrm{Y}\mathfrak{gl}_{2} \displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathrm{Y}\mathfrak{gl}_{2},\qquad \displaystyle\varepsilon:\mathrm{Y}\mathfrak{gl}_{2} \displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}, \displaystyle t_{ij}(u) \displaystyle\longmapsto\sum_{k=1,2}t_{ik}(u)\otimes t_{kj}(u), \displaystyle T(u) \displaystyle\longmapsto T^{-1}(u), \displaystyle T(u) \displaystyle\longmapsto 1. There is an embedding \iota:U\mathfrak{sl}_{2}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Y}\mathfrak{gl}_{2} given by (2.26) \begin{aligned} h&\longmapsto t_{11}^{(1)}-t_{22}^{(1)},\\ e&\longmapsto t_{12}^{(1)},\\ f&\longmapsto t_{21}^{(1)}.\end{aligned}\qquad Here e,h,f are the standard generators of U\mathfrak{sl}_{2} . We often write simply U\mathfrak{sl}_{2}\subset\mathrm{Y}\mathfrak{gl}_{2} for the image of this embedding. There is a homomorphism of associative algebras depending on a parameter a\in\mathbb{C} defined by (2.27) \displaystyle\mathrm{ev}_{a}:\mathrm{Y}\mathfrak{gl}_{2} \displaystyle\longrightarrow U\mathfrak{gl}_{2}, \displaystyle t_{ij}(u) \displaystyle\longmapsto\left(\delta_{ij}1+\frac{E_{ij}}{u+a}\right). Here E_{ij} , i,j=1,2 , are the standard generators of U\mathfrak{gl}_{2} . Let \mathrm{qdet}(u)=t_{11}(u)t_{22}(u{-}1)-t_{21}(u)t_{12}(u{-}1) be the quantum determinant. Coefficients of \mathrm{qdet}(u) generate the center \mathcal{Z}\mathrm{Y}\mathfrak{gl}_{2} of \mathrm{Y}\mathfrak{gl}_{2} (see [ CP95 ] ). It can be verified that \Delta(\mathrm{qdet}(u))=\mathrm{qdet}(u)\otimes\mathrm{qdet}(u),\qquad S(\mathrm{qdet}(u))=\mathrm{qdet}(u)^{-1}. The algebra \mathrm{Y}\mathfrak{sl}_{2} is the quotient Hopf algebra (2.28) \mathrm{Y}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}=\mathrm{Y}\mathfrak{gl}_{2}/\left(\mathrm{qdet}(u)-1\right). We denote \bar{t}^{(r)}_{ij}\in\mathrm{Y}\mathfrak{sl}_{2} and \bar{t}^{(r)}_{ij}(u)\in\mathrm{Y}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}[[u^{-1}]] the images of generators of \mathrm{Y}\mathfrak{gl}_{2} and corresponding formal series, respectively. Composition of the inclusion \iota with the quotient map gives an injective homomorphism of Hopf algebras \bar{\iota}:\ U\mathfrak{sl}_{2}\longmapsto\mathrm{Y}\mathfrak{sl}_{2} . Let \varphi(u)\in\mathcal{Z}\mathrm{Y}\mathfrak{gl}_{2}[[u^{-1}]] be such that \varphi(u)\varphi(u-1)\mathrm{qdet}(u)=1,\qquad\varphi(\infty)=1. We have \Delta(\varphi(u))=\varphi(u)\otimes\varphi(u) and there is an injective homomorphism of Hopf algebras. \displaystyle\mathfrak{i}:\mathrm{Y}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Y}\mathfrak{gl}_{2}, \displaystyle\bar{t}_{ij}(u)\longmapsto\varphi(u)t_{ij}(u). Composition of an evaluation homomorphism \mathrm{ev}_{a} with \mathfrak{i} gives a homomorphisms of associative algebras \mathrm{Y}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}\longrightarrow\mathrm{U}\mathfrak{gl}_{2} which we denote by \overline{\mathrm{ev}}_{a} . There is a family of shift automorpisms \tau_{a} of \mathrm{Y}\mathfrak{gl}_{2} parameterized by a complex number a , (2.29) \displaystyle\tau_{a}:\mathrm{Y}\mathfrak{gl}_{2}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Y}\mathfrak{gl}_{2}, \displaystyle t_{ij}(u)\longmapsto t_{ij}(u+a). Note that the ideal (\mathrm{qdet}(u)-1) is preserved by \tau_{a} , therefore this automorphism descends to an automorphism of \mathrm{Y}\mathfrak{sl}_{2} which we denote by \bar{\tau}_{a} . To answer questions about submodule structure of a representation of \mathrm{Y}\mathfrak{sl}_{2} computationally, it is convenient to use finite sets of generators. Proposition 2.9 . For any i,j\in\{1,2\} , the set \{\bar{t}_{11}^{(1)}-\bar{t}_{22}^{(1)},\bar{t}_{12}^{(1)},\bar{t}_{21}^{(1)},\bar{t}_{ij}^{(2)}\} generates \mathrm{Y}\mathfrak{sl}_{2} . Proof. Denote Y_{ij} the subalgebra of \mathrm{Y}\mathfrak{sl}_{2} generated by \{\bar{t}_{11}^{(1)}-\bar{t}_{22}^{(1)},\bar{t}_{12}^{(1)},\bar{t}_{21}^{(1)},\bar{t}_{ij}^{(2)}\} . Write \mathrm{qdet}(u)=\sum_{s=1}^{\infty}\mathrm{qdet}_{s}\ u^{-s} . Then (2.30) \mathrm{qdet}_{s}=\bar{t}_{11}^{(m)}+\bar{t}_{22}^{(m)}+\textit{lower terms}. Let i=j . From ( 2.30 ) with m=2 , we conclude that \bar{t}_{11}^{(2)}\in Y_{ij} and \bar{t}_{22}^{(2)}\in Y_{ij} . Observe that (2.31) [\bar{t}_{12}^{(1)},\bar{t}_{22}^{(2)}]=[\bar{t}_{12}^{(1)},\bar{t}_{22}^{(2)}]-[\bar{t}_{12}^{(0)},\bar{t}_{22}^{(3)}]=\bar{t}_{22}^{(0)}\bar{t}_{12}^{(2)}-\bar{t}_{22}^{(2)}\bar{t}_{12}^{(0)}=\bar{t}_{12}^{(2)}-\bar{t}_{22}^{(2)}, [\bar{t}_{21}^{(1)},\bar{t}_{22}^{(2)}]=[\bar{t}_{21}^{(1)},\bar{t}_{22}^{(2)}]-[\bar{t}_{21}^{(0)},\bar{t}_{22}^{(3)}]=\bar{t}_{21}^{(0)}\bar{t}_{22}^{(2)}-\bar{t}_{21}^{(2)}\bar{t}_{22}^{(0)}=\bar{t}_{22}^{(2)}-\bar{t}_{21}^{(2)}. Hence \bar{t}_{12}^{(2)}\in Y_{ij} and \bar{t}_{21}^{(2)}\in Y_{ij} . Let i\neq j . Without loss of generality, we assume i=1,j=2 . Then [\bar{t}_{21}^{(1)},\bar{t}_{12}^{(2)}]=[\bar{t}_{21}^{(1)},\bar{t}_{12}^{(2)}]-[\bar{t}_{21}^{(0)},\bar{t}_{12}^{(3)}]=\bar{t}_{11}^{(0)}\bar{t}_{22}^{(2)}-\bar{t}_{22}^{(0)}\bar{t}_{11}^{(2)}=\bar{t}_{11}^{(2)}-\bar{t}_{22}^{(2)}. From ( 2.30 ) with m=2 , we conclude that \bar{t}_{11}^{(2)}\in Y_{ij} and \bar{t}_{22}^{(2)}\in Y_{ij} . It follows that \bar{t}_{21}^{(2)}\in Y_{ij} . Thus, in all cases we have \bar{t}_{kl}^{(2)}\in Y_{ij} for all k,l,i,j . Suppose \bar{t}_{kl}^{(s)}\in Y_{ij} for all s\leq N . Then [\bar{t}_{12}^{(2)},\bar{t}_{21}^{(N)}]=[\bar{t}_{12}^{(1)},\bar{t}_{21}^{(N+1)}]+\bar{t}_{22}^{(1)}\bar{t}_{11}^{(N)}-\bar{t}_{22}^{(N)}\bar{t}_{11}^{(1)}=\left([\bar{t}_{12}^{(0)},\bar{t}_{21}^{(N+2)}]+\bar{t}_{22}^{(0)}\bar{t}_{11}^{(N+1)}-\bar{t}_{22}^{(N+1)}\bar{t}_{11}^{(0)}\right)+\\ +\bar{t}_{22}^{(1)}\bar{t}_{11}^{(N)}-\bar{t}_{22}^{(N)}\bar{t}_{11}^{(1)}=\bar{t}_{11}^{(N+1)}-\bar{t}_{22}^{(N+1)}+\textit{lower terms}. Using ( 2.30 ) with m=N+1 , \bar{t}_{11}^{(N+1)},\bar{t}_{22}^{(N+1)}\in Y_{ij} . Then (2.32) [\bar{t}_{12}^{(1)},\bar{t}_{22}^{(N+1)}]=[\bar{t}_{12}^{(1)},\bar{t}_{22}^{(N+1)}]-[\bar{t}_{12}^{(0)},\bar{t}_{22}^{(N+2)}]=\bar{t}_{22}^{(0)}\bar{t}_{12}^{(N+1)}-\bar{t}_{22}^{(N+1)}\bar{t}_{12}^{(0)}=\bar{t}_{12}^{(N+1)}, [\bar{t}_{21}^{(1)},\bar{t}_{22}^{(N+1)}]=[\bar{t}_{21}^{(1)},\bar{t}_{22}^{(N+1)}]-[\bar{t}_{21}^{(0)},\bar{t}_{22}^{(N+2)}]=\bar{t}_{21}^{(0)}\bar{t}_{22}^{(N+1)}-\bar{t}_{21}^{(N+1)}\bar{t}_{22}^{(0)}=-\bar{t}_{21}^{(N+1)}. Therefore, \bar{t}_{kl}^{(N+1)}\in Y_{ij} . ∎ Categories of finite-dimensional representations of \mathrm{Y}\mathfrak{sl}_{2} and of U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} are equivalent, see [ GT16 ] . In the paper we mainly use U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} . We believe that the corresponding consideration for the Yangian are essentially equivalent. However, for brute force computer computations, it is often advantageous to use \mathrm{Y}\mathfrak{sl}_{2} . 3. Known results on representations of U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} . We collect some useful facts about U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} -modules and supply some proofs. 3.1. Properties of modules over a Hopf algebra. We use properties of U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} -modules induced by the Hopf algebra structure. Let \mathrm{H} be a Hopf algebra over \mathbb{C} with \Delta,\eta,\epsilon,S being comultiplication, unit, counit and antipode respectively. All modules of \mathrm{H} we consider are assumed to be finite-dimensional. The trivial module of \mathrm{H} is vector space \mathbb{C} with a\in\mathrm{H} acting on \mathbb{C} by \epsilon(a) . Let \mathrm{V} be an \mathrm{H} -module. Since (\epsilon\otimes\mathrm{id})\circ\Delta=(\mathrm{id}\otimes\epsilon)\circ\Delta=\mathrm{id} , for an \mathrm{H} -module \mathrm{V} , we have \mathrm{V}\otimes\mathbb{C}\cong\mathbb{C}\otimes\mathrm{V}\cong\mathrm{V} as \mathrm{H} -modules. Denote by \rho_{\mathrm{V}} the corresponding homomorphism \mathrm{H}\to\mathrm{End}_{\mathbb{C}}\left(\mathrm{V}\right) . The left dual module \mathrm{V}^{*} is the dual vector space on which a\in\mathrm{H} acts by \rho_{\mathrm{V}}(S(a))^{*} . The right dual module {}^{*}\mathrm{V} is the dual vector space on which a\in\mathrm{H} acts by \rho_{\mathrm{V}}(S^{-1}(a))^{*} . We have ({}^{*}\mathrm{V})^{*}\cong{}^{*}(\mathrm{V}^{*})\cong\mathrm{V} . We often call left dual module simply dual module. The following properties are standard exercises in the theory of Hopf algberas. Lemma 3.1 . Let \mathrm{V} , \mathrm{W} and \mathrm{U} be \mathrm{H} -modules. Then there are canonical isomorphisms of \mathrm{H} -modules (3.1) \displaystyle\left(\mathrm{V}\otimes\mathrm{W}\right)^{*} \displaystyle\cong\mathrm{W}^{*}\otimes\mathrm{V}^{*}, (3.2) \displaystyle{}^{*}\hskip-2.0pt\left(\mathrm{V}\otimes\mathrm{W}\right) \displaystyle\cong{}^{*}\mathrm{W}\otimes{}^{*}\mathrm{V}.\hskip 15.0pt{} In addition there are canonical isomorphisms vector spaces (3.3) \displaystyle\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{H}}\left(\mathrm{V}\otimes\mathrm{W},\mathrm{U}\right) \displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{H}}\left(\mathrm{V},\mathrm{U}\otimes\mathrm{W}^{*}\right), (3.4) \displaystyle\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{H}}\left(\mathrm{W}\otimes\mathrm{V},\mathrm{U}\right) \displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{H}}\left(\mathrm{V},{}^{*}\mathrm{W}\otimes\mathrm{U}\right). We supply the proof of Lemma 3.1 in the Appendix A . The following corollary is immediate. Corollary 3.2 . Let \mathrm{V} and \mathrm{W} be \mathrm{H} -modules. Then there are canonical isomorphisms of vector spaces (3.6) \mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{H}}\left(\mathrm{V},\mathrm{W}\right)\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{H}}\left(\mathrm{W}^{*},\mathrm{V}^{*}\right)\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{H}}\left(\mathbb{C},\mathrm{W}\otimes\mathrm{V}^{*}\right). ∎ We also use the following standard fact, Lemma 3.3 . Let \mathrm{M} be an \mathrm{H} -module. • Functor \mathrm{Hom}(\mathrm{M},\cdot) is exact from the left. • Functors \mathrm{M}\otimes\cdot and \cdot\otimes\mathrm{M} are exact In other words, given a short exact sequence of \mathrm{H} -modules \mathrm{U}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{V}\twoheadrightarrow\mathrm{W}, there are exact sequences \displaystyle\mathrm{Hom}(\mathrm{M},\mathrm{U}) \displaystyle\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Hom}(\mathrm{M},\mathrm{V})\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}(\mathrm{M},\mathrm{W}), \displaystyle\mathrm{M}\otimes\mathrm{U} \displaystyle\hookrightarrow\mathrm{M}\otimes\mathrm{V}\twoheadrightarrow\mathrm{M}\otimes\mathrm{W}, \displaystyle\mathrm{U}\otimes\mathrm{M} \displaystyle\hookrightarrow\mathrm{V}\otimes\mathrm{M}\twoheadrightarrow\mathrm{W}\otimes\mathrm{M}. ∎ Given an algebra automorphism \varphi:\mathrm{H}\longrightarrow\mathrm{H} and an \mathrm{H} module \mathrm{V} , let \mathrm{V}_{\varphi} be the module twisted by \varphi . In other words, \mathrm{V}_{\varphi}=\mathrm{V} as a vector space and any a\in\mathrm{H} acts on \mathrm{V}_{\varphi} by \rho_{\mathrm{V}}(\varphi(a)) . The following lemma is obvious. Lemma 3.4 . Let \mathrm{V},\mathrm{W} be \mathrm{H} -modules and \varphi a Hopf algebra automorphism of \mathrm{H} . Then \displaystyle(\mathrm{V}\otimes\mathrm{W})_{\varphi} \displaystyle\cong\mathrm{V}_{\varphi}\otimes\mathrm{W}_{\varphi}, \displaystyle(\mathrm{V}^{*})_{\varphi} \displaystyle\cong(\mathrm{V}_{\varphi})^{*}, \displaystyle\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{H}}\left(\mathrm{V}_{\varphi},\mathrm{W}_{\varphi}\right) \displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{H}}\left(\mathrm{V},\mathrm{W}\right). ∎ We end this section with another simple fact. Lemma 3.5 . Let \hat{\omega} be an isomorphism of Hopf algebras (\mathrm{H},\Delta) and (\mathrm{H},\Delta^{\textit{op}}) . Let \mathrm{V},\mathrm{W} be \mathrm{H} -modules. Then (3.7) \displaystyle(\mathrm{V}\otimes\mathrm{W})_{\hat{\omega}} \displaystyle\cong\mathrm{W}_{\hat{\omega}}\otimes\mathrm{V}_{\hat{\omega}}, (3.8) \displaystyle\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{H}}(\mathrm{V}_{\hat{\omega}},\mathrm{W}_{\hat{\omega}}) \displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{H}}(\mathrm{V},\mathrm{W}). Proof. Equations ( 3.7 ), ( 3.8 ) are straightforward, cf. Lemma 3.4 . ∎ 3.2. Duals and shifts. The dual of an evaluation module is an evaluation module with shifted evaluation parameter. Lemma 3.6 . We have (3.9) [\alpha,\beta]^{*}\cong[\alpha+2,\beta+2]. More generally, ([\alpha_{1},\beta_{1}][\alpha_{2},\beta_{2}]\dots[\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}])^{*}\cong[\alpha_{n}+2,\beta_{n}+2]\dots[\alpha_{2}+2,\beta_{2}+2][\alpha_{1}+2,\beta_{1}+2]. Proof. Denote \mathrm{V}=[\alpha,\alpha+2,\beta] and recall evaluation parameter a=\frac{\alpha+\beta}{2} . Note that S(K)=K^{-1} which implies that the set of U_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2} -weights for the module \mathrm{V}^{*} is the same as for the module \mathrm{V} . This means that \mathrm{V}=\mathrm{V}^{*} as U_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2} -modules. It remains to note that \rho_{\mathrm{V}^{*}}(f_{0})=\rho_{\mathrm{V}}(S(f_{0}))^{*}=-\rho_{\mathrm{V}}(K^{-1}f_{0})^{*}=-\rho_{\mathrm{V},U_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}(K^{-1}q^{-a}e)^{*}=\\ =q^{-a-2}\rho_{\mathrm{V},U_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}(-eK^{-1})^{*}=\rho_{\mathrm{V}}(S(e_{1}))^{*}=\rho_{\mathrm{V}^{*}}(q^{-a-2}e_{1}). Similarly, \rho_{\mathrm{V}^{*}}(e_{0})=\rho_{\mathrm{V}^{*}}(q^{a+2}f_{1}). The general case follows from Lemma 3.1 . ∎ Recall the shift automorphisms \tau_{a} of Hopf algebra U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} , see Section 2.2 . Lemma 3.7 . The twist of an evaluation module by a shift automorphism \tau_{a} is the evaluation module with shifted parameter, [\alpha,\beta]_{\tau_{a}}\cong[\alpha+a,\beta+a]. More generally, ([\alpha_{1},\beta_{1}][\alpha_{2},\beta_{2}]\dots[\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}])_{\tau_{a}}\cong[\alpha_{1}+a,\beta_{1}+a][\alpha_{2}+a,\beta_{2}+a]\dots[\alpha_{n}+a,\beta_{n}+a]. In particular, \mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{H}}(\mathbb{C},[\alpha_{1},\beta_{1}]\dots[\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}])=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{H}}(\mathbb{C},[\alpha_{1}+a,\beta_{1}+a]\dots[\alpha_{n}+a,\beta_{n}+a]). Proof. The first statement follows from the identity \mathrm{ev}_{a}\circ\tau_{b}=\mathrm{ev}_{a+b} . The rest follows from Lemma 3.4 and \mathbb{C}_{\tau_{a}}\cong\mathbb{C} . ∎ 3.3. Lattice restrictions. Let \mathcal{L}=2\mathbb{Z}+\frac{2\pi\mathrm{i}}{\log(q)}\mathbb{Z} be the lattice. If the evaluation parameters of two evaluation modules are not equal modulo \mathcal{L} , then these modules are in general position. In particular, their tensor products in different orders are irreducible and isomorphic. Therefore, in any tensor product of evaluation modules we can order tensor factors by the class of evaluation parameter. In other words any product of evaluation modules \mathrm{V} can be written in the form (3.10) \mathrm{V}\cong\mathrm{V}_{(a_{1})}\otimes\mathrm{V}_{(a_{2})}\otimes\dots\otimes\mathrm{V}_{(a_{k})}, where \mathrm{V}_{(a_{j})} is product of all evaluation modules with parameters equal a_{j} modulo \mathcal{L} and a_{i} are distinct modulo \mathcal{L} . Here \mathrm{V}_{(a_{i})} can be a trivial module. It is clear that the product is commutative \mathrm{V}_{(a_{j})}\otimes\mathrm{V}_{(a_{j+1})}\cong\mathrm{V}_{(a_{j+1})}\otimes\mathrm{V}_{(a_{j})} . To study spaces of homomorphisms of U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} -modules, it is sufficient to consider the case k=1 , that is it is sufficient to consider tensor products of evaluation modules with evaluation parameters equal modulo \mathcal{L} . Moreover, by Lemma 3.7 all choices of \mathcal{L} -coset are equivalent, so, one can assume a_{1}=0 . Such a reduction is a known idea, see e.g. [ EM03 ] . We formulate the precise statement and give a proof which illustrates our methods. Proposition 3.8 . Let \mathrm{V} , \mathrm{W} be products of evaluation U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} -modules \mathrm{V}=\mathrm{V}_{(a_{1})}\otimes\dots\otimes\mathrm{V}_{(a_{k})},\;\;\mathrm{W}=\mathrm{W}_{(a_{1})}\otimes\dots\otimes\mathrm{W}_{(a_{k})}, as in ( 3.10 ). Then there is an isomorphism of vector spaces (3.11) \mathrm{Hom}_{U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}}\left(\mathrm{V},\mathrm{W}\right)\cong\bigotimes_{l=1}^{k}\mathrm{Hom}_{U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}}\left(\mathrm{V}_{(a_{l})},\mathrm{W}_{(a_{l})}\right). Proof. We prove ( 3.11 ) by induction on k . Base k=1 is tautological. Assume the statement for tensor products ( 3.11 ) with k-1 factors. Denote \bar{\mathrm{V}}=\mathrm{V}_{(a_{2})}\otimes\dots\otimes\mathrm{V}_{(a_{k})} , \bar{\mathrm{W}}=\mathrm{W}_{(a_{2})}\otimes\dots\otimes\mathrm{W}_{(a_{k})} . By Lemma 3.1 , \mathrm{Hom}_{U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}}\left(\mathrm{V},\mathrm{W}\right)\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}}\left(\mathrm{W}_{(a_{1})}^{*}\otimes\mathrm{V}_{(a_{1})},\bar{\mathrm{W}}\otimes\bar{\mathrm{V}}^{*}\right). Note that the evaluation parameters of factors of \mathrm{V}_{(a_{i})}^{*} , \mathrm{W}_{(a_{i})}^{*} equal to a_{i} modulo \mathcal{L} . Let \varphi\in\mathrm{Hom}_{U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}}\big(\mathrm{W}_{(a_{1})}^{*}\otimes\mathrm{V}_{(a_{1})},\bar{\mathrm{W}}\otimes\bar{\mathrm{V}}^{*}\big) . The image of \varphi is a submodule of \mathrm{W}_{(a_{1})}^{*}\otimes\mathrm{V}_{(a_{1})} isomorphic to a quotient module of \bar{\mathrm{W}}\otimes\bar{\mathrm{V}}^{*} . The only possible common monomial of q -characters, \chi_{q}(\mathrm{W}_{(a_{1})}^{*}\otimes\mathrm{V}_{(a_{1})}) and \chi_{q}(\bar{\mathrm{W}}\otimes\bar{\mathrm{V}}^{*}) is 1 . It follows that the q -character of the image of \varphi is 1 with some multiplicity. Let \mathrm{U} be an U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} -module such that \chi_{q}(\mathrm{U})=m\cdot 1 for m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0} . Then U is a direct sum of trivial modules, \mathrm{U}=\oplus_{i=0}^{m}\mathbb{C} . Indeed, e_{i},f_{i} act by zero in \mathrm{U} and K is diagonalizable in any finite-dimensional U_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2} -module. This implies that \varphi belongs to the image of embedding (3.12) \mathrm{Hom}_{U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}}\left(\mathrm{W}_{(a_{1})}^{*}\otimes\mathrm{V}_{(a_{1})},\mathbb{C}\right)\otimes\mathrm{Hom}_{U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}}\left(\mathbb{C},\bar{\mathrm{W}}\otimes\bar{\mathrm{V}}^{*}\right)\longrightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}}\left(\mathrm{W}_{(a_{1})}^{*}\otimes\mathrm{V}_{(a_{1})},\bar{\mathrm{W}}\otimes\bar{\mathrm{V}}^{*}\right), given by composition. Hence, ( 3.12 ) is actually an isomorphism. Finally, by Lemma 3.1 , \displaystyle\mathrm{Hom}_{U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}}\left(\mathrm{W}_{(a_{1})}^{*}\otimes\mathrm{V}_{(a_{1})},\mathbb{C}\right) \displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}}\left(\mathrm{V}_{(a_{1})},\mathrm{W}_{(a_{1})}\right), \displaystyle\mathrm{Hom}_{U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}}\left(\mathbb{C},\bar{\mathrm{W}}\otimes\bar{\mathrm{V}}^{*}\right) \displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}}\left(\bar{\mathrm{V}},\bar{\mathrm{W}}\right). Applying the induction hypothesis we obtain ( 3.11 ). ∎ In particular, Proposition 3.8 asserts that \mathrm{Hom}_{U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}}\left(\mathbb{C},\mathop{\otimes}_{l=1}^{k}\mathrm{W}_{(a_{l})}\right)\cong\bigotimes_{l=1}^{k}\mathrm{Hom}_{U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}}\left(\mathbb{C},\mathrm{W}_{(a_{l})}\right). We have formulated Proposition 3.8 for tensor products of evaluation modules to serve the needs of this text. Clearly, one can formulate and prove in the same way a similar statement for arbitrary finite-dimensional U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} modules. Proposition 3.8 can be strengthen. We discuss such possibilities in Sections 4.5 and 11.1 . From now on we always assume (unless directly said otherwise) that all our evaluation parameters are even integers. In other words, we always assume that the evaluation parameters are zero modulo \mathcal{L} . 3.4. Tensor products of two evaluation modules. As we know, tensor products of evaluation modules in general position are irreducible. In this section we give a description of all tensor products of two evaluation modules in non-general position obtained in [ CP91 ] . We give a proof using q -characters and an explicit form of R -matrix. Proposition 3.9 . Let \alpha_{1},\beta_{1} \alpha_{2},\beta_{2} be four even integers such that \alpha_{1}<\alpha_{2}\leq\beta_{1}+2\leq\beta_{2} . Then there are two non-split short exact sequences (3.13a) [\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}-4][\beta_{1}+4,\beta_{2}]\hookrightarrow[\alpha_{1},\beta_{1}][\alpha_{2},\beta_{2}]\twoheadrightarrow[\alpha_{1},\beta_{2}][\alpha_{2},\beta_{1}], (3.13b) [\alpha_{1},\beta_{2}][\alpha_{2},\beta_{1}]\hookrightarrow[\alpha_{2},\beta_{2}][\alpha_{1},\beta_{1}]\twoheadrightarrow[\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}-4][\beta_{1}+4,\beta_{2}]. Proof. First we use q -characters to find the composition factors of [\alpha_{1},\beta_{1}][\alpha_{2},\beta_{2}] . Note that products [\alpha_{1},\beta_{2}][\alpha_{2},\beta_{1}] and [\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}-4][\beta_{1}+4,\beta_{2}] are in general position and therefore they are irreducible. The highest monomials of the q -characters of modules [\alpha_{1},\beta_{1}][\alpha_{2},\beta_{2}] and [\alpha_{2},\beta_{1}][\alpha_{1},\beta_{2}] coincide, 1_{\alpha_{1}}1_{\alpha_{1}+2}\dots 1_{\beta_{1}}1_{\alpha_{2}}\dots 1_{\beta_{2}}=\left(1_{\alpha_{1}}1_{\alpha_{1}+2}\dots 1_{\beta_{1}}1_{\beta_{1}+2}\dots 1_{\beta_{2}}\right)\left(1_{\alpha_{2}}\dots 1_{\beta_{1}}\right). Moreover, the difference \chi_{q}([\alpha_{1},\beta_{1}])\chi_{q}([\alpha_{2},\beta_{2}])-\chi_{q}([\alpha_{1},\beta_{2}][\alpha_{2},\beta_{1}]) contains exactly one dominant monomial, \left(1_{\alpha_{1}}1_{\alpha_{1}+2}\dots 1_{\alpha_{2}-4}1_{\alpha_{2}}^{-1}\dots 1_{\beta_{1}+2}^{-1}\right)\left(1_{\alpha_{2}}1_{\alpha_{2}+2}\dots 1_{\beta_{2}}\right)=\left(1_{\alpha_{1}}1_{\alpha_{1}+2}\dots 1_{\alpha_{2}-4}\right)\left(1_{\beta_{1}+4}\dots 1_{\beta_{2}}\right), see Example 2.2 . Therefore, this difference coincides with \chi_{q}([\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}-4][\beta_{1}+4,\beta_{2}]) . Hence, the products [\alpha_{1},\beta_{2}][\alpha_{2},\beta_{1}] and [\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}-4][\beta_{1}+4,\beta_{2}] form the composition factors of [\alpha_{1},\beta_{1}][\alpha_{2}\dots\beta_{2}] . The module [\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}-4][\beta_{1}+4,\beta_{2}] is a submodule of [\alpha_{1},\beta_{1}][\alpha_{2}\dots\beta_{2}] . Indeed, by Proposition 2.7 there exists a U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} -homomoprhism [\alpha_{1},\beta_{1}][\alpha_{2}\dots\beta_{2}]\longrightarrow[\alpha_{2},\beta_{2}][\alpha_{1}\dots\beta_{1}] with non-trivial kernel which does not contain a vector of the highest \mathfrak{sl}_{2} -weight. Therefore, the kernel of this map is a submodule isomorphic to [\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}-4][\beta_{1}+4,\beta_{2}] . Similarly, the module [\alpha_{1},\beta_{2}][\alpha_{2},\beta_{1}] is a submodule of [\alpha_{2},\beta_{2}][\alpha_{1}\dots\beta_{1}] . It remains to check that the sequences ( 3.13 ) do not split. One sequence is a shifted dual of the other, see Section 3.1 . Therefore, if one of sequences splits, so does the other. If both modules [\alpha_{1},\beta_{1}][\alpha_{2},\beta_{2}] and [\alpha_{2},\beta_{2}][\alpha_{1},\beta_{1}] were direct sums of two simple modules, then the space of U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} -homomorphisms between them would be two-dimensional in contradiction to Proposition 2.7 . ∎ We illustrate the short exact sequence [0][14,16]\hookrightarrow[0,10][4,16]\twoheadrightarrow[4,10][0,16], which is a particular case of ( 3.13a ), by the following picture. 0 2 4 6 8 10 12 14 16 0 2 4 6 8 10 12 14 16 0 2 4 6 8 10 12 14 16 On this picture each circle corresponds to a variable 1_{a} where the horizontal coordinate is given by a . The variables corresponding to the filled circles aligned horizontally form the dominant monomial of one of the factors. The factors are ordered from top to bottom. We show by the dashed lines and non-filled circles the variables which are cancelled during q -character multiplication. The quotient module on the right is a result of vertical reordering of variables 1_{a} which produces a product of strings in general position. 4. Basics of trivial submodules. In this paper we call arbitrary tensor products of 2-dimensional evaluation modules with evaluation parameters in 2\mathbb{Z} by words, see Section 4.1 below. A major goal of this paper is to study the spaces \mathrm{Hom}_{U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}}(w_{1},w_{2}) for arbitrary words w_{1},w_{2} . By Corollary 3.2 it is equivalent to the study of the spaces \mathrm{Hom}_{U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}}(\mathbb{C},w) . Denote H(w)=\mathrm{Hom}_{U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}}(\mathbb{C},w),\qquad h(w)=\dim(H(w)). The spaces H(w) and the numbers h(w) are one of the main objects of study in this text. The space H(w) is canonically identified with the space of \ell -singular vectors of \ell -weight 1 in w . We do not make distinction between these two spaces. 4.1. Words and arcs. We develop the language related to products of two-dimensional evaluation modules. We start from combinatorial objects related to study of tensor powers of \mathrm{L}_{1} . Fix some n\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0} . Let i,j\in\{1,\dots,2n\} , we call an ordered pair (i,j) with i<j an uncolored arc. For an uncolored arc (i,j) , we call i (respectively j ) left (respectively right) end of (i,j) . We say that the arc (i,j) connects to i and to j . For a set of uncolored arcs C=\{(i_{k},j_{k})\} , denote \mathrm{LE}(C)=\{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}\},\;\;\mathrm{RE}(C)=\{j_{1},\dots,j_{n}\} the sets of left and right ends of arcs in C , respectively. We call a pair of uncolored arcs (i_{1},j_{1}),(i_{2},j_{2}) intersecting if i_{1}<i_{2}<j_{1}<j_{2} or i_{2}<i_{1}<j_{2}<j_{1} . We call a set of uncolored arcs \{(i_{1},j_{1}),\dots,(i_{n},j_{n})\} an uncolored arc configuration if \{1,\dots,2n\}=\mathop{\bigsqcup}_{k=1}^{n}\{i_{k},j_{k}\}. Given an uncolored arc configuration C=\{(i_{1},j_{1}),\dots,(i_{n},j_{n})\} we say that letters i_{k} and j_{k} of w are connected by an arc in C . We call an uncolored arc configuration connected if for any two arcs \alpha,\beta there exists a sequence (\alpha=\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{m}=\beta) , such that for all i\in\{0,\dots,m-1\} arcs \alpha_{i} and \alpha_{i+1} intersect. We call an uncolored arc configuration C=\{(i_{1},j_{1}),\dots,(i_{n},j_{n})\} an uncolored Catalan arc configuration if any two pairs of arcs in that configuration do not intersect. There is a convenient pictorial description for uncolored Catalan arc configurations. For instance, there are five uncolored Catalan arc configurations for n=3 given by the following picture. , , , , Note that an uncolored Catalan arc configuration C is uniquely defined by the set of the left ends \mathrm{LE}(C) (or by the set of the right ends \mathrm{RE}(C) ). We denote the set of uncolored arcs, uncolored arc configurations and uncolored Catalan arc configurations as \mathrm{UArc}(2n),\;\mathrm{UConf}(2n),\;\mathrm{UCatConf}(2n) . For a given C=\{(i_{k},j_{k})\}_{k=1}^{n}\in\mathrm{UConf}(2n) and a set of m arcs S\subset C define a new arc configuration \mathring{C}_{S}\in\mathrm{UConf}(2(n-m)) obtained by removing arcs in S from C . Formally, let C\backslash S=\{(i_{k}^{\prime},j_{k}^{\prime})\}_{k\in\{1,\dots,n-m\}} . Denote \displaystyle i_{k}^{\prime\prime}=i_{k}^{\prime}-|((\mathrm{LE}(S)\cup\mathrm{RE}(S))\cap\{1,\dots,i_{k}^{\prime}-1\})|, \displaystyle j_{k}^{\prime\prime}=j_{k}^{\prime}-|((\mathrm{LE}(S)\cup\mathrm{RE}(S))\cap\{1,\dots,j_{k}^{\prime}-1\})|, and set \mathring{C}_{S}=\{(i_{k}^{\prime\prime},j_{k}^{\prime\prime})\}_{k\in\{1,\dots,n-m\}}\in\mathrm{UConf}(2(n-m)) . Note that if C\in\mathrm{UCatConf}(2n) , then \mathring{C}_{S}\in\mathrm{UCatConf}(2(n-m)) . Uncolored arc configurations are suited to study singular U_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2} singular vectors in \mathrm{L}_{1}^{\otimes n} . Now we introduce combinatorial objects related to U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} singular vectors in products of two-dimensional evaluation modules. We denote the product of two-dimensional evaluation modules [a_{1},a_{1}]\dots[a_{k},a_{k}] by (a_{1},\dots,a_{k}) . We call the sequence of even integers w=(a_{1},\dots,a_{k}) a word w . Often we do not distinguish between a word w=(a_{1},...,a_{k}) and U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} -module [a_{1},a_{1}]\dots[a_{k},a_{k}] . For a word w=(a_{1},\dots,a_{k}) we write simply a_{1}a_{2}\dots a_{k} . In particular, in all examples in the present work all a_{i} are one-digit even numbers, so there is no confusion. For a word w=(a_{1},...,a_{k}) , we call k=l(w) length of w . We use the notation W_{k}=\left(2\mathbb{Z}\right)^{\times k} for the set of all words of the length k . The permutation group S_{k} acts on the set W_{k} by permutations of letters. Due to Lemma 3.7 , h(w) does not change if we shift all letters of w by the same number. This allows to consider words modulo overall shift of all letters by 2\mathbb{Z} . It is clear that if l(w) is odd, then h(w)=0 , so we restrict our attention to the cases when l(w) is even. Given a word w=(a_{1},\dots,a_{k}) and a letter b\in 2\mathbb{Z} , we denote I_{w}(b)=\{j\in\{1,\dots,k\}\ |\ a_{j}=b\} . We call the set \mathrm{supp}(w)=\{b\in 2\mathbb{Z}\ |\ I_{w}(b)\neq\varnothing\} the support of the word w . We call a word w connected if \mathrm{supp}(w) is a segment in 2\mathbb{Z} , that is if \mathrm{supp}(w)=\{a,a+2,\dots,a+b\} for some a,b\in 2\mathbb{Z} . For a word w=(a_{1},\dots,a_{k}) , we call the multiset \{a_{1},\dots,a_{k}\} the content of w and denote it \mathrm{Cont}(w) . For a word w=(a_{1},...,a_{k}) we call w^{*}=(a_{k}+2,a_{k-1}+2,...,a_{1}+2) the dual word, For a pair of words w_{1}=(a_{1},\dots,a_{k})\in W_{k},\;\;w_{2}=(b_{1},\dots,b_{l})\in W_{l} we write w_{1}w_{2} for the concatenation (a_{1},\dots,a_{k},b_{1},\dots,b_{l}) . Let w=(a_{1},\dots,a_{k}) and \tilde{w}=(b_{1},\dots,b_{l}) be words. We call \tilde{w} is a subword of w if \tilde{w} is obtained from w by deleting some letters, in other words if b_{j}=a_{i_{j}} for some increasing sequence 1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}<\dots<i_{l}\leq k . In the case of w=w_{1}\tilde{w}w_{2} , that is if i_{j+1}=i_{j}+1 for all 1\leq j\leq l-1 , we call \tilde{w} a factor of w . We use notation a^{n} for the word (a,\dots,a)=a\dots a , where a is repeated n times. Given two words w_{1},w_{2} we call a word w_{3} a shuffle of w_{1} and w_{2} if w_{3} contains w_{1} as a subword and removing this subword from w_{3} gives w_{2} . Definition 4.1 . For a word w=(a_{1},...,a_{2n}) we call an uncolored arc (i,j)\in\mathrm{UArc}(2n) an arc of color b if a_{j}-1=a_{i}+1=b . We denote the set of arcs of a word w of a color b by \mathrm{Arc}_{b}(w) and we denote \mathrm{Arc}(w)=\mathop{\bigsqcup}_{b\in 2\mathbb{Z}+1}\mathrm{Arc}_{b}(w). We call elements of \mathrm{Arc}(w) arcs in the word w . A motivation for Definition 4.1 is given by the following lemma. Lemma 4.2 . Module w=(a_{1},...,a_{k}) is irreducible if and only if \mathrm{Arc}(w)=\mathrm{Arc}(w^{*})=\varnothing . Proof. The lemma follows directly from the description of irreducible modules, see Section 2.2 . ∎ The concept of arcs is useful for other reasons as well and we will extensively use it. Definition 4.3 . For a word w of length 2n , we call a set of arcs \{(i_{1},j_{1}),\dots,(i_{n},j_{n})\}\subseteq\mathrm{Arc}(w) an arc configuration if \{1,\dots,2n\}=\mathop{\bigsqcup}_{k=1}^{n}\{i_{k},j_{k}\}. We denote by \mathrm{Conf}(w) the set of all arc configurations of a word w . Lemma 4.4 . Let w be a word such that the set \mathrm{Conf}(w) is non-empty. Then the number m_{b} of arcs of the color b in C\in\mathrm{Conf}(w) does not depend on C and equals (4.1) m_{b}=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{j}|I_{w}(b-1-2j)|=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{j}|I_{w}(b+1+2j)|. Proof. If b=\min(\mathrm{supp}(w))+1 , then m_{b}=|I_{w}(b-1)| . If b=\min(\mathrm{supp}(w))+3 , then clearly m_{b}=|I_{w}(b-1)|-|I_{w}(b-3)| . The first equation of the lemma follows by induction on b since m_{b}=|I_{w}(b-1)|-m_{b-2} . The second equation of the lemma is similar. ∎ Clearly, \mathrm{Arc}(w)\subset\mathrm{UArc}(l(w)),\;\;\mathrm{Conf}(w)\subset\mathrm{UConf}(l(w)) , so the language developed above for uncolored arcs (uncolored arc configurations) is applicable to arcs (arc configurations). Note also that if C\in\mathrm{Conf}(w) , then \mathring{C}_{S}\in\mathrm{Conf}(\mathring{w}_{S}) , where \mathring{w}_{S} is the word w with letters in positions \mathrm{LE}(S)\cup\mathrm{RE}(S) removed. We denote \mathrm{CatConf}(w)=\mathrm{UCatConf}(l(w))\cap\mathrm{Conf}(w) . 4.2. Vectors corresponding to Catalan arc configurations. We denote by H_{2n} the space \mathrm{Hom}_{U_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}\big(\mathbb{C},\mathrm{L}_{1}^{\otimes{2n}}\big) . The space H_{2n} is naturally identified with the subspace of U_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2} singular vectors of weight 0 in (\mathrm{L}_{1})^{\otimes 2n} . For a word w\in W_{2n} , clearly H(w)\subseteq\mathrm{Hom}_{U_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}\left(\mathbb{C},w\right)=H_{2n}. It is well known that \dim\left(H_{2n}\right)=\frac{1}{n+1}\binom{2n}{n}=C_{n} is the n th Catalan number. There is the following combinatorial description for a basis of H_{2n} . Let \{(+),(-)\} be a basis of U_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2} -module \mathrm{L}_{1} such that f(+)=(-) , e(-)=(+) , K(+)=q(+) , K(-)=q^{-1}(-) . Let \{(\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{k})\ |\ \epsilon_{i}\in\{+,-\}\} be the basis for U_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2} -module \mathrm{L}_{1}^{\otimes k} , where (\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{k})=(\epsilon_{1})\otimes\dots\otimes(\epsilon_{k}) . We call this basis standard. We order the basis of \mathrm{L}_{1} by (+)>(-) and extend this order to a basis of \mathrm{L}_{1}^{\otimes n} lexicographically. For a non-zero vector v\in\mathrm{L}_{1}^{\otimes{n}} , write (4.2) v=a(\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{n})+\textit{lower terms},\qquad a\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}. Then we call (\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{n}) the leading term of v . For an arc (i,j)\in\mathrm{Arc}(2n) , define an operator A_{(i,j)} acting in \mathrm{L}_{1}^{\otimes 2n} by A_{(i,j)}=1-q^{-1}P_{ij}, where P_{ij} is the operator of permutation of the i th and j th tensor factors. Let C=\{(i_{k},j_{k})\}_{k=1}^{n}\in\mathrm{UCatConf}(2n) . Define a vector (4.3) v_{C}=A_{(i_{1},j_{1})}\dots A_{(i_{n},j_{n})}\epsilon_{C}. Here \epsilon_{C}=(\epsilon_{1}(C),\dots,\epsilon_{2n}(C)) , \epsilon_{i_{k}}(C)=(+) and \epsilon_{j_{k}}(C)=(-) for all k . Note that if two arcs (i_{1},j_{1}),(i_{2},j_{2}) do not have common ends, then the operators A_{(i_{1},j_{1})},A_{(i_{2},j_{2})} commute. Moreover, A_{(1,2)}(+,-) is the unique up to proportionality singular vector of weight zero in \mathrm{L}_{1}^{\otimes 2} . Since C is a Catalan arc configuration, it follows that the vector v_{C} is singular. Moreover, the leading term of v_{C} is \epsilon_{C} . Therefore, \{v_{C}\}_{C\in\mathrm{UCatConf}(2n)} is a basis of H_{2n} . We call this basis the Catalan basis. Lemma 4.5 . Let w be a word w\in W_{2n} and C\in\mathrm{CatConf}(w) , then v_{C}\in H(w). Proof. We proceed by induction on n . For the case n=1 we have just to check that (+-)-q^{-1}(-+) is annihilated by f_{0} . Indeed, (f_{0}\otimes 1+K\otimes f_{0})((+-)-q^{-1}(-+))=q^{1-a-2}(++)-q^{-1}q^{-a}(++)=0. Now, assume that the statement is known for the case n-1 . There exists an arc in C of the form (i,i+1) . Let \iota:\mathbb{C}\longrightarrow(a_{i},a_{i}+2) be an embedding of U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} -modules sending 1\mapsto A_{12}(+,-) . Then \iota_{i}=\mathrm{Id}_{(a_{1},\dots,a_{i-1})}\otimes\iota\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{(a_{i+2},\dots,a_{2n})}:(a_{1},\dots,\hat{a}_{i},\hat{a}_{i+1},\dots,a_{2n})\longrightarrow(a_{1},\dots,a_{2n}) is also an embedding of U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} -modules. Recall that \mathring{C}_{\{(i,i+1)\}} is C with the arc (i,i+1) removed. Then v_{C}=\iota_{i}(v_{\mathring{C}_{\{(i,i+1)\}}}) . By the induction hypothesis v_{\mathring{C}_{(i,i+1)}} is an \ell -singular vector. Therefore, v_{C} is also an \ell -singular vector. ∎ Since the set of vectors \{v_{C}\}_{C\in\mathrm{CatConf}(2n)} is linearly independent, we obtain a lower bound for the dimension h(w) of \mathrm{Hom}_{U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}}(\mathbb{C},w) . Corollary 4.6 . For any word w\in W_{2n} , (4.4) h(w)\geq|\mathrm{CatConf}(w)|. ∎ 4.3. Action of \check{R} -matrix. The R -matrix in standard basis of ab=\mathrm{L}_{1}\otimes\mathrm{L}_{1} is explicitly given by (4.5) \check{R}(a,b)=\begin{pmatrix}q^{a-b}-q^{2}&0&0&0\\ 0&q^{a-b}-q^{a-b+2}&q^{a-b+1}-q&0\\ 0&q^{a-b+1}-q&1-q^{2}&0\\ 0&0&0&q^{a-b}-q^{2}\end{pmatrix}. Let \check{R}_{i,2n}(a,b)=\mathrm{Id}_{\mathrm{L}_{1}^{\otimes(i-1)}}\otimes\check{R}(a,b)\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{\mathrm{L}_{1}^{\otimes(2n-i-1)}} be the R -matrix acting in the i th and (i+1) st factors of \mathrm{L}_{1}^{\otimes 2n} . For all a,b , the operator \check{R}(a,b) is a homomorphism of U_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2} -modules. In particular, the R -matrix \check{R}_{i,2n}(a,b) acts on multiplicity space H_{2n}=\mathrm{Hom}_{U_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}\left(\mathbb{C},\mathrm{L}_{1}^{\otimes 2n}\right) . We describe this action in the basis of uncolored Catalan arc configurations. The action of \check{R}_{i,2n}(a,b) affects only arcs which have an end in \{i,i+1\} . There are 4 cases which we describe in Lemma 4.7 . In pictures, we do not show arcs which are not affected by the action. We write parameters a and b respectively on i th and (i+1) st positions on the left and on (i+1) st and i th places on the right, to indicate the evaluation parameters of the U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} modules. \lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}. Proof. The lemma is proved by a direct computation. ∎ 4.4. Slide equivalence. Definition 4.8 . Define a map \displaystyle s:W_{k} \displaystyle\longrightarrow W_{k}, \displaystyle(a_{1},\dots,a_{k}) \displaystyle\longmapsto(a_{2},a_{3},\dots,a_{k},a_{1}+4). We call the map s the slide. We call words related by a sequence of slides, inverses of slides and shift automorphisms slide equivalent. Lemma 4.9 . Let w\in W_{k} be a word. There is a canonical isomorphism, (4.6) H(w)\cong H(s(w)). Proof. By Lemma 3.1 , H(w)=\mathrm{Hom}_{U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}}(\mathbb{C},a_{1}\dots a_{k})\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}}(a_{1}^{*},a_{2}\dots a_{k})\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}}(\mathbb{C},a_{2}\dots a_{k}(a_{1}^{*})^{*}). Since a^{*}\cong a+2 , the lemma follows. ∎ For any word w\in W_{2n} , the map s induces a bijection s_{\mathrm{Conf}(w)}:\mathrm{Conf}(w)\rightarrow\mathrm{Conf}(s(w)) on arc configurations given by s_{\mathrm{Conf}(w)}:\{(1,j_{1}),(i_{2},j_{2}),\dots,(i_{n},j_{n})\}\mapsto\{(j_{1}-1,2n),(i_{2}-1,j_{2}-1),\dots,(i_{n}-1,j_{n}-1)\}. This bijection preserves the Catalan arc configurations: if C\in\mathrm{CatConf}(w) , then s_{\mathrm{Conf}(w)}(C)\in\mathrm{CatConf}(s(w)) . Moreover, for any C\in\mathrm{CatConf}(w) , isomorphism ( 4.6 ) identifies v_{C} with \alpha v_{s_{\mathrm{Conf}{w}}(C)} , where \alpha\in\mathbb{C}^{\times} depends on a choice of isomorphism [a,a]^{*}\cong[a+2,a+2] . We describe another map on W_{2n} which preserves h(w) . Let \hat{\omega}:U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}\longrightarrow U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} be the map defined on the Jimbo-Drinfeld generators by (4.7) \displaystyle\hat{\omega}:U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} \displaystyle\longrightarrow U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}, \displaystyle K^{\pm 1} \displaystyle\longmapsto K^{\mp 1}, \displaystyle e_{i} \displaystyle\longmapsto f_{i},\;\;i\in\{0,1\}, \displaystyle f_{i} \displaystyle\longmapsto e_{i},\;\;i\in\{0,1\}. Lemma 4.10 . The map \hat{\omega} is an isomorphism of Hopf algebras and that [\alpha,\beta]_{\hat{\omega}}\cong[-\beta,-\alpha] . Proof. The lemma is checked by a direct computation. ∎ Corollary 4.11 . Define an anti-involution \omega on W_{n} by \omega:(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})\longmapsto(-a_{n},\dots,-a_{1}) . Then (4.8) H(w)\cong H(\omega(w)). Proof. For a two-dimensional evaluation module (a) we have (a)_{\hat{\omega}}=(-a) . By ( 3.7 ), we obtain ((a_{1},\dots,a_{n}))_{\hat{\omega}}\cong(-a_{n},\dots,-a_{1}) . For the counit \epsilon we have \epsilon\circ\hat{\omega}=\epsilon . Therefore, \mathbb{C}_{\hat{\omega}}\cong\mathbb{C} . By ( 3.8 ), this implies ( 4.8 ). ∎ 4.5. The tensor product structure of H(w) for non-connected words w . In Section 3.3 , we discussed the tensor product structure of spaces of homomorphisms when evaluation parameters belong to different lattices. Here we give a tensor product decomposition for the case of one lattice. A word w is not connected if and only if w is a shuffle of two non-empty words of the form w_{1}=(a_{1},\dots,a_{k})\in W_{k},w_{2}=(b_{1},\dots,b_{l})\in W_{l} such that b_{i}\geq a_{j}+4 for all i,j . Proposition 4.12 . Let word w be a shuffle of two non-empty words w_{1}=(a_{1},\dots,a_{k})\in W_{k},w_{2}=(b_{1},\dots,b_{l})\in W_{l} such that b_{i}\geq a_{j}+4 for all i,j . Then H(w)\cong H(w_{1})\otimes H(w_{2}),\qquad h(w)=h(w_{1})h(w_{2}). Proof. Recall that ab\cong ba unless |a-b|=2 , see Section 2.2 . Therefore, letters of w_{1} commute with letters of w_{2} . Thus, w\cong w_{2}\otimes w_{1} . By Lemmas 3.1 and 3.6 , H(w)\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}}(\mathbb{C},w_{2}\otimes w_{1})\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}}((a_{k}-2,\dots,a_{1}-2),w_{2}). Similarly to the proof of Proposition 3.8 the q -character of (a_{k}-2,\dots,a_{1}-2) is a sum of monomials of variables 1_{a} with a<d and q -character of w_{2} is a sum of monomials of variables 1_{b} with b>d , where d=\max_{i}\{a_{i}\}+2 . This implies that the map \mathrm{Hom}_{U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}}((a_{k}-2,\dots,a_{1}-2),\mathbb{C})\otimes\mathrm{Hom}_{U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}}(\mathbb{C},w_{2})\longrightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}}((a_{k}-2,\dots,a_{1}-2),w_{2}), induced by composition is an isomorphism. Applying Lemma 3.1 once again, we get \mathrm{Hom}_{U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}}((a_{k}-2,\dots,a_{1}-2),\mathbb{C})\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}}(\mathbb{C},(a_{1},\dots,a_{k}))=H(w_{1}). The proposition follows. ∎ Remark 4.13 . Similarly to Proposition 4.12 , one can show a more general statement. Namely, let \mathrm{V}_{1} , \mathrm{V}_{2} , \mathrm{W}_{1} \mathrm{W}_{2} be U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2} -modules. Assume that if a positive power of 1_{a} appears in \chi_{q}(\mathrm{W}_{1}) or \chi_{q}(\mathrm{V}_{1}) and a positive power of 1_{b} appears in \chi_{q}(\mathrm{W}_{2}) or \chi_{q}(\mathrm{V}_{2}) , then b\geq a+4 . Then \mathrm{Hom}_{U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}}(\mathrm{V}_{1}\otimes\mathrm{V}_{2},\mathrm{W}_{1}\otimes\mathrm{W}_{2})=\mathrm{Hom}_{U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}}(\mathrm{V}_{1},\mathrm{W}_{1})\otimes\mathrm{Hom}_{U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}}(\mathrm{V}_{2},\mathrm{W}_{2}). Proposition 4.12 reduces the computation of H(w) to the case of connected words. We expect that one can further strengthen the definition of connected words preserving Proposition 4.12 . We discuss this issue in Section 11.1 . 5. Bounds for h(w) . 5.1. Lower bound. In this section we give a lower estimate for h(w)=\dim(\mathrm{Hom}_{U_{q}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}}(\mathbb{C},w)) for a word w\in W_{2n} in terms of \mathrm{Conf}(w) which improves the bound by the number of Catalan arc configurations ( 4.4 ). Let C\in\mathrm{Conf}(w) and let (i_{1},j_{1}),(i_{2},j_{2}) be two intersecting arcs in C . Definition 5.1 . Assume i_{1}<i_{2}<j_{1}<j_{2} . We call intersection of arcs (i_{1},j_{1}),(i_{2},j_{2}) irreducible if a_{i_{2}}\notin\{a_{i_{1}},a_{j_{1}}\} and reducible otherwise. We call C\in\mathrm{Conf}(w) an irreducible arc configuration if all intersections of arcs in C are irreducible. For a word w\in W_{2n} denote the subset of irreducible arc configurations \mathrm{IConf}(w)\subseteq\mathrm{Conf}(w) . Clearly, \mathrm{CatConf}(w)\subset\mathrm{IConf}(w) . A configuration obtained by removing arcs from an irreducible configuration is irreducible. Irreducible arc configurations are compatible with slides. Proposition 5.2 . Let w\in W_{2n} be a word and C\in\mathrm{Conf}(w) an arc configuration. Then C irreducible if and only if s_{\mathrm{Conf}(w)}(C) is irreducible. Proof.
